Parameterized Algebraic Protocols
Andreia Mordido, Janek Spaderna, Peter Thiemann, Vasco T. Vasconcelos

Abstract
We propose algebraic protocols that enable the definition of protocol templates and session types analogous to the definition of domain-specific types with algebraic datatypes. Parameterized algebraic protocols subsume all regular as well as most context-free and nested session types and, at the same time, replace the expensive superlinear algorithms for type checking by a nominal check that runs in linear time. Algebraic protocols in combination with polymorphism increase expressiveness and modularity by facilitating new ways of parameterizing and composing session types.



1
Introduction

The advantages of modeling data with algebraic datatypes (ADTs)
have been recognised since the 80s

Burstall 1977
,
Burstall et al. 1980
.
ADTs are easy to define and use, they enable the construction of
domain-specific types, and are amenable to efficient nominal type checking.
Functional programming languages quickly embraced ADTs along
with the concept of pattern matching which is still gaining momentum.

Algebraic datatypes integrate recursive types, sum types, product
types, and generativity (each ADT definition creates a new type) in a
single declaration. The smooth integration of different typing concepts
makes ADTs easy to learn and use. It also simplifies the
implementation of type checkers because they can elide superlinear algorithms for checking
equivalence of equirecursive types (where recursive types are
considered equal to their unfolding) in favor of a linear comparison of nominal types.

We show that the ADT approach for defining recursive
datatypes can be carried over to

algebraic protocols
(AP), a novel method for defining a large class
of recursive protocols. Analogous to ADTs, APs integrate
concepts from the theory of session types

Honda 1993
,
Takeuchi et al. 1994

(recursive types, choice types, and sequence types) and generativity
in a single declaration. Analogous to ADTs, APs are easy to use and
are amenable to efficient type checking.

We develop APs by analogy to ADTs. Consider the following ADT for lists of
integers.

⬇

data

IntList

=

Nil

|

Cons

Int

IntList

The datatype
IntList
comes with two data constructors,

Nil
to construct an
empty list and
Cons
to construct a list from an element
of type
Int
and a list of type
IntList
. The
alternative between the constructors corresponds to a sum type and the
two arguments of
Cons
give rise to a product
type. Clearly,
IntList
is a recursive type. Moreover, its
definition introduces
IntList
as a new type, different
from any other. Type equivalence is simple: we just compare ADTs
by name.

Although
IntList
specifies a (static) data type,
it carries an implicit dynamics and it should be
quite natural for the reader to imagine elements of this type as
finite sequences of messages between two communication partners: if
there are further elements, the sender selects the
constructor
Cons
, sends a value of type

Int
and then recurses. If there are no further elements,
the sender selects
Nil
and stops the transmission.
On the other end, the receiver awaits a constructor. On receiving a

Cons
it receives an
Int
and recurses. It stops
on receiving a
Nil
.

This view has striking parallels to the theory of binary session
types
Honda 1993
,
Honda et al. 1998
,
Takeuchi et al. 1994
.
Session types provide a rich type discipline for communication channels that
statically guarantees protocol fidelity as well as type soundness across
communication channels. Here is a session type for the protocol associated to
the
IntList
ADT, written in the syntax of existing
systems
Almeida et al. 2022
,
Lindley and Morris 2017
,
Gay and Vasconcelos 2010

and as seen from the sender’s perspective:
1
1

1

Writing

IntListS
for the session type corresponding to

IntList
.

⬇

type

IntListS

=

\mu
\alpha
.

\oplus
{
Cons
:

!
Int
.
\alpha
,

Nil
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
}

The leading
\mu\alpha\dots
constructs an equirecursive type. The body is
an internal choice type
\oplus
with two alternatives indicated by the tags

Cons
and
Nil
. If the sender selects

Cons
, it must continue according to

!
Int
.
\alpha
, which means it must send an integer and then
recurse. If the sender selects
Nil
, then the protocol
comes to an end as indicated by the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
.
Type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
represents a channel endpoint ready to be closed. The dual
type,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}}
, represents the other endpoint, waiting to be closed.
As the name
suggests, a session type describes a full session of interactions
between the communication partners up to the end.

Like this example, most session type systems
rely on structural typing and equirecursion;
this way, unfolding does not give rise to communication.
Traditional session type systems restrict
recursion to the tail position (as in
IntListS
), so that types are effectively finite
automata with regular trace languages. Consequently, type equivalence
and subtyping for such systems are decidable in polynomial time. Recently, richer forms of recursion
have been considered that lift the restriction to tail recursion, so
that types are akin to, e.g., deterministic context-free grammars

Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
,
Padovani 2017
,
Padovani 2019
,
Almeida et al. 2022
,
Das et al. 2021
.
Unfortunately, when types are endowed with richer forms
of recursion, type equivalence gets more complex
(doubly-exponential
Das et al. 2021
).
The implementation of a type equivalence algorithm for these systems is nontrivial

Almeida et al. 2020
; incomplete algorithms have been
used to speed up type equivalence
Das et al. 2021
;
annotations have been proposed
Padovani 2019

that make type checking effective. But the algorithms for
type equivalence remain asymptotically superlinear in all these works.

The sad truth is that structural session types are quite verbose and
too complex for the average programmer, thus hampering their adoption. While equirecursive types are
expressive, understanding their equivalence is hard for programmers
and inefficient in language implementations.

What if programming with expressive recursive protocols were as easy as
programming with ADTs? The theory and practice of algebraic protocols
that we propose in this paper delivers exactly such an
approach. Enhanced with parameters and polymorphism in the style of
System F, APs provide a uniquely simple and efficient approach to modularity and abstraction
when defining and implementing communication protocols.

Here is the declaration of an algebraic
protocol corresponding to the session type
IntListS
:
2
2

2

Writing

IntListP
for the protocol type corresponding to

IntList
.

⬇

protocol

IntListP

=

Nil

|

Cons

Int

IntListP

The keyword
protocol
, in place of
data
, denotes an
algebraic
protocol
type rather than an algebraic
data
type. A
protocol type is like a template that describes a composable segment of a
session. It neither describes a full session, nor is it committed to a
particular direction of communication. Rather, a protocol type has to be

materialized
into a session type by assigning a direction and specifying
a continuation session: we write
!
IntListP
.
S
to send a sequence of
integers and continue with session type
S
or
?
IntListP
.
S

to receive a sequence of integers and continue with
S
, so that the
session type
IntListS
corresponds to
!
IntListP
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
.
Hence, protocol types relate to session types just like composable continuations
relate to continuations
Felleisen 1988
,
Danvy and Filinski 1992
: the
former describe a segment of interaction (execution) while the latter describe
interaction (execution) to the end.

Just like ADTs, we can equip APs with parameters as in

ListP

a
:

⬇

protocol

ListP

a

=

Nil

|

Cons

a

ListP

While parameters range over types in ADT definitions, they
range over
protocols
in AP definitions! This facility is key to
the modularity of APs. For example, the protocol

ListP

Q
repeats the protocol
Q
. It is
also possible to flip the direction of
Q
by considering the protocol

ListP

-
Q
: repeat the protocol
Q
but with reversed roles
of sender and receiver (more in
section

2.2
). With this
modular design, we can define finite streams of
anything expressible as an algebraic protocol. The
external and internal choices in previous session type systems are
captured by the sum-of-product nature of algebraic types—the sum
type replaced by internal/external choice and the product type
replaced by sequencing as in the linear logic
interpretation of session types
Caires et al. 2016
.

Algebraic protocol types are nominal and isorecursive.
This combination of features reduces the complexity of type equivalence from
asymptotically doubly-exponential
Almeida et al. 2020
,
Das et al. 2021

to linear. Notably, the expressivity of algebraic protocols
is on par with that of session types with non-regular recursion

Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
,
Das et al. 2021
,
so all protocols expressible in such theories can be adapted
to algebraic protocols (without paying the price of hosting complex or
incomplete algorithms in the compiler). Interestingly, types
are now also iso-associative, thus saving us from defining
hard-coded associativity laws, due to the basic properties
of sequential composition

Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
,
Almeida et al. 2022
.

Contributions

•

Algebraic protocols (AP). We propose a new approach for defining
recursive protocols compositionally inspired by parameterized algebraic
datatypes. This approach combines naming, recursion, and
choice-of-sequence types analogously to algebraic
datatypes. It adds a novel notion of direction to the component
protocols of a tag. Checking type equivalence becomes simple and efficient
(worst case linear in the size of the types) as APs are nominal.

•

Algebraic session types (AlgST). We define a core calculus that is based
on linear System F with (non-linear) recursion and features session types
based on APs. Type checking for AlgST is specified by bidirectional typing
rules that have a direct algorithmic reading.
We prove type soundness via preservation and
progress.

•

Full implementation as a publicly available artifact

Spaderna et al. 2023
, including practical extensions (cf.
section

5
).

Overview

Section

2
contains an informal introduction of algebraic
protocols with examples; we illustrate the gain in
expressivity and modularity with respect to other work in

section

2.3
.
Section

3
discusses the type structure
of AlgST and establishes key results for efficient type checking: correctness of type normalization and
worst case complexity of type equivalence.
Section

4
defines
statics and dynamics of expressions
and processes followed by a presentation of the metatheoretical results.

Section

5
discusses the implementation; since all rules are
algorithmic, their appropriate reading leads directly to a type checker and an interpreter.

Sections

6
and
7
discuss related work and conclude.
Proofs, auxiliary results, further examples, an informal outline of an embedding
of context-free session types to AlgST, and further discussion are provided as supplementary material. The
implementation is publicly available
Spaderna et al. 2023
.

2
Algebraic Protocols and session types

This section provides a programmer’s view of algebraic session types (AlgST). It is driven by examples and
introduces the concepts of algebraic protocols and session types. Our
syntax is inspired by Haskell, as implemented in our artifact. Examples make
liberal use of practical extensions of the formal system (cf.
section

5
).

2.1
Algebraic datatypes as protocols

Suppose we want to transmit abstract syntax trees for a language with integers
and addition. Their type might be defined by an algebraic datatype definition as
in Haskell or ML.

⬇

data

Ast

=

Con

Int

|

Add

Ast

Ast

This declaration defines the type
Ast
along with the types of the constructor
functions, which can be used to construct
Ast
values as well as for pattern matching
on them:

⬇

Con

:

Int

\rightarrow

Ast

Add

:

Ast

\rightarrow

Ast

\rightarrow

Ast

The type
Ast
is recursive and its
Add

constructor takes two
Ast
values as arguments.
To define a protocol for transmitting values of type
Ast
, we merely change the keyword

to

as in

⬇

protocol

AstP

=

ConP

Int

|

AddP

AstP

AstP

This declaration defines the protocol type
AstP
along with tags
ConP

and
AddP
, which serve to introduce and eliminate branching in the protocol.
The resulting
AstP
protocol can be used in
two directions,
!
AstP
for sending and
?
AstP
for receiving
data.
3
3

3

The implementation allows us to reuse a

data
declaration as a
protocol
declaration,
thus overloading the constructors.

Protocol types only describe behavior, they do not classify run-time values. They inhabit a
dedicated kind
P
.

We illustrate the
AstP
protocol with a function to send an

Ast
value. We write functions
in
channel passing style
, which takes a channel as its argument
and returns it after processing some prefix of the channel’s session
type. The function’s type is universally quantified over the continuation
type

of the unexplored part of the channel.
The argument
type adds some prefix to

that describes the part of the
session performed by the function. In our example the prefix is
!

The square brackets indicate type abstraction (on the left) and type

The use of pattern matching with

As usual in session type systems, channel values of session type are linear

The

a tag and branches according to it. Branching also “pushes” transmission of the fields on the
channel type analogous to the

⬇

Left of the turnstile

before the

c
after matching against the selectors
ConP
and
AddP

The

(with the type on the left) consumes the linear
binding for channel

. The pattern
ConP

reintroduces variable

, binds it with the channel
after processing the selection and gives it an updated type (on the right) for the body of the
corresponding branch. The consideration for pattern
AddP

c

is analogous. The choice of the branch is external and depends
on the received selector tag.

The protocol in this subsection goes beyond the reach of most session type systems because
they only support tail recursive session types.
Thus, functions like
sendAst
and
recvAst
have
no meaningful type in traditional
session type systems
Gay and Vasconcelos 2010
,
Lindley and Morris 2017
.
However, they can be written using context-free or nested session
types
Almeida et al. 2022
,
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
,
Das et al. 2021
. We
discuss the differences in
section

6
.

2.2
Simple protocols with polarities

The protocol type
AstP
from

describes unidirectional
communication. This section extends protocol types with

polarities
to model bidirectional communication.
The protocol type
Arith
of an arithmetic server (cf.
Gay and Hole 1999
) serves as an example. The server takes a command,
Neg
or

Add
, accepts one or two inputs to the commands and produces its output.

⬇

=

Neg

-
Int

|

Int

-

The new element in this declaration is the

“

reversal of the direction of communication. A client would use this protocol in “forward
direction”, that is, in a session type of the form
!

.

whereas the server would

.

Ordinary types like

2.3
Parameterized protocols and modularity

Algebraic protocols can have protocol parameters, just like algebraic data
types have type parameters. This facility provides a powerful and versatile approach for declaring
modular protocols.
As a first example, we declare a protocol that repeatedly runs
another protocol:

⬇

protocol

Stream

a

=

Next

a

(
Stream

a
)

Using this protocol we define a process that sends infinitely many ones on a channel:

⬇

ones

:

!
Stream

Int
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}

\rightarrow

Unit

ones

c

=

select

Next

[
Int
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
]

c

\rhd

sendInt

[!
Stream

Int
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
]

1

\rhd

ones

When using the
Stream
protocol, the continuation session
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
does not matter because the protocol
never reaches the continuation. For a parameterized protocol,

select
takes type parameters corresponding to the protocol
parameters (
Int
) as well as the continuation session (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
).
Algebraic protocols sidestep issues with contractiveness of
recursive definitions and thus guardedness of the protocols: we always
have to make explicit progress by selecting the
Next
tag.

Up to this point, the examples we presented are definable with some
effort in previous work on session types. The following examples
exploit features that make AlgST stand out:

•

Traditional session types lack composability in the sense that
abstraction over behavior is only possible in the tail
position. Polymorphic context-free session types

Almeida et al. 2022
lift this restriction, but
at the price of a type equivalence
algorithm that takes doubly exponential time in the
worst-case. AlgST allows abstraction of any part of a protocol
while providing a linear-time algorithm for type equivalence.

•

The protocol kind is a unique feature of AlgST that enables
abstraction over entire subprotocols and their orientation at any place in a
protocol. This facility enables us to define a toolbox of generic
protocol operators, inspired by regular language operations, and
flip between client and server views using the negation operator.

Generic servers

Parametric protocols enable the construction of generic servers that abstract over the
subsidiary protocols. Our first example created a stream of integers,
but what if we wanted a server for
Stream

Arith
that runs the
Arith
protocol ad infinitum?
To this end we write a generic server,
stream
, that implements
the
Stream

a
protocol, for any
a
, and invokes a subsidiary server for the
sub-protocol
a
. We abstract the type of this
sub-server with a parameterized type alias:

⬇

type

Service

a

=

\forall
(
s
:
S
).

?
a
.
s

\rightarrow

s

Thus armed, we can write the generic server and use it to run

Stream

Arith
.

⬇

stream

:

\forall
(
a
:
P
).

Service

a

\rightarrow

?
Stream

a
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}

\rightarrow

Unit

stream

[
a
]

serve

c

=

match

c

with

{

Next

c

\rightarrow

serve

[?
Stream

a
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
]

c

\rhd

stream

[
a
]

serve

}

streamArith

:

?
Stream

Arith
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}

\rightarrow

Unit

streamArith

=

stream

[
Arith
]

serveArith

We call the server constructed in this way
passive
as it waits to receive a
Next

tag before running the subsidiary protocol. Dually, we can define an

active
server.

Active servers — an exercise in duality and negation

An active server offers the

Next
tag to its client, indicating its readiness to accept a
request. Such a server generalizes the
ones
server.
All we need to do is flip two bits in the type:

⬇

streamAct

:

\forall
(
a
:
P
).

Service

a

\rightarrow

!
Stream

-
a
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}

\rightarrow

Unit

[
a
]

svc

c

=

select

Next

[-
a
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
]

c

\rhd

svc

[!
Stream

-
a
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
]

\rhd

[
a
]

svc

Why do we have to write
!

-
a
in the type?
One might expect the active server to run on a channel
whose type is the dual of
?

.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
, which is

!
Stream

3
Types

The type structure underlying AlgST comprises three parts: the kind
structure, the type language and protocol definitions. The type
language and protocol definitions are mutually recursive. To
simplify the exposition, the formalized language is based on linear
System F. While we formalize protocol
definitions, we do not formalize the standard treatment of (linear)
algebraic datatypes and pattern matching, although we use it in examples.

The type language encompasses types with different features
(as illustrated in
section

2
).
For a correct type abstraction, we need to endow the system with a classification
of types through kinds.
AlgST distinguishes three kinds.

s

classifies session types, which in turn classify
endpoints of communication channels.

t

classifies all types that classify run-time
values.

p

classifies protocol types. The kind
p
subsumes any other
kind by an implicit lifting that associates a type with a protocol
suitable to transmit it. A protocol type per se classifies pure
behavior, but no run-time values.

Kinds are linearly ordered in a subkinding relation, which is the
reflexive transitive closure of the strict ordering
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}
. Type formation includes a standard kind
subsumption rule.

Here is the grammar of types:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\to U}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\otimes U}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}{.T}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}\;\mid\;

functional types

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{S}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{S}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {S}}}\;\mid\;

session types

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}\penalty\ {\overline{T}}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-T}}

protocol types

We mostly use the metavariable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}
to range over session
types (kind
s
). Metavariables
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
range over functional types
(kind
t
) and protocol types (kind
p
).
Functional types comprise the usual types of linear System F: unit,
function, product, universal quantification, and type variables.
The session type operators are receiving, sending,
passive and active termination, and the dual operator. The dual
operator swaps the direction of all communications in the spine of a
session type. Thus, a session type is a
sequence of communications either terminated by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}}
or by

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
, or extensible with a type variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
of kind
s
.

The protocol type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}\penalty\ {\overline{T}}}}
refers to the protocol declared as

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}
with parameters
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}
. The
operator “
-
” reverses the direction of communication of its
protocol parameter. While duality transforms session types
outside-in, the reverse operator transforms protocols inside-out.

A kind context
\Delta
maps type variables to kinds and protocol
names to first-order arrow kinds that determine the
number of parameters of the protocol.
Formally, we should write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\varepsilon}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}
for the kind of a
protocol constructor without parameters, but we often abbreviate it to
p
.

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\;::=\;\;\cdot\;\mid\;\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}\;\mid\;\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}}}}

Type formation (synthesis, check-against)

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
,
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}

\frac{}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{Unit}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}\quad\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\to U}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{Arrow}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}\quad\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\otimes U}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{Pair}} }\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}\in\Delta}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{Var}} }\par\frac{\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}{.T}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{Poly}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\quad\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?T.S}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{In}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\quad\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!T.S}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{Out}} }\par\begin{minipage}[56.9055pt]\begin{equation*}\frac{}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{End?}} }\par\frac{}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{End!}} {S}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{Dual}} }\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}}}}\in\Delta\quad\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}}\Leftarrow{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}\penalty\ {\overline{T}}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{Protocol}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{-T}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{MsgNeg}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}^{\prime}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{{T}-{Sub}} }

Figure 1
:
Algorithmic type formation rules

Figure

1
contains the type formation rules. We
present them in algorithmic form from the beginning. The judgment

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
assigns to type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
its minimal kind
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}
under kind
context
\Delta
. The corresponding checking judgment
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
takes
inputs
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}
and checks if the synthesized kind is a
subkind of the expected one.

The types for unit (
T-Unit
), functions
(
T-Arrow
), pairs (
T-Pair
), and universal
polymorphism (
T-Poly
)
have kind
t
like their constituents. Variables have any kind
that has been assigned to them (
T-Var
).
AlgST’s notion of session type distinguishes passive
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}}
and active

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
termination of a session (
T-End?
,
T-End!
), and the
dual operator (
T-Dual
). Conventional session types require types for
the values exchanged in messages; here we ask for protocols instead
(
T-In
and
T-Out
).
Rule
T-Protocol
builds a protocol type from a protocol name

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}
. We assume that the kind context contains a binding for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}
that
determines its arity. All parameters to a protocol must be protocols
themselves. The latter constitutes no restriction because any type can be
lifted to a protocol. A protocol type can change direction using the reverse operator (rule
T-MsgNeg
).

Algebraic protocol types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}\penalty\ {\overline{\alpha}}}}
are defined analogously to algebraic
datatypes.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{protocol}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}\ \overline{\alpha}}}\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{=}}\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}_{i}}}\ \overline{T_{i}}}\}_{i\in I}}}

This declaration defines the protocol type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}
that takes as
many parameters as indicated by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}
. The protocol has choices
indexed by
i\in I
. Each choice is tagged by a
selector tag

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}_{i}}}

that guards a sequence of subprotocols
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T_{i}}}}
, to be processed in that
order. We assume that selector tags are globally unique. Program may use any
full application
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}\penalty\ {\overline{U}}}}
as a protocol type.
We check a single protocol declaration using the formation rules where
\Delta
may
refer to previously defined protocols:
4
4

4

For mutually
recursive protocols
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}}}
, we add the
assumptions for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}}}
when checking each individual
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}_{i}}}
.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{protocol}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}\ \overline{\alpha}}}\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{=}}\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}_{i}}}\ \overline{T_{i}}}\}_{i\in I}}}\quad\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\overline{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T_{i}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\quad(\forall i\in I)}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}}

Type conversion

\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}

\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{Refl}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{Sym}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}\quad\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{3}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{3}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{Trans}} }\par\frac{}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{DualEnd?}} }\par\frac{}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{DualEnd!}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\quad\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{S}}})}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {S}}})}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{DualIn}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\quad\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{S}}})}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {S}}})}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{DualOut}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {S}}})}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{DualInv}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{Sub}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\quad\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?(-T)}.{S}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{S}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{NegIn}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\quad\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!(-T)}.{S}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{S}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{NegOut}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-(-T)}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{{C}-{NegInv}} }

Figure 2
:
Type conversion (congruence rules omitted)

Types obey a kind-indexed conversion
relation.
Figure

2
presents the declarative
rules. Conversion is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive
(
C-Refl
,
C-Sym
,
C-Trans
).
We define duality as a conversion on session types. The rules entail
that duality changes the direction of transmissions from the outside
(
C-DualEnd?
,
C-DualEnd!
,

C-DualIn
,
C-DualOut
)
and that duality is involutory (
C-DualInv
). Kind subsumption is lifted to conversion (
C-Sub
). The last line
governs the interaction between the reverse operator and the session
type constructors: reverse flips the direction from the inside
(
C-NegIn
,
C-NegOut
). Reverse is
also involutory (
C-NegInv
). We omit the standard congruence rules.

While type formation is algorithmic, the declarative definition of
type conversion is more perspicuous. For the
upcoming algorithmic expression typing relation we define a normalization
algorithm for types, which is sound and complete with respect to the
declarative rules.

In a type in normal form, the reverse operator can only occur at most once at the top level (of
a type of kind
p
) and at the top level of the parameters of a protocol type. The

Dual
operator only appears on type variables, at the end of
the spine of a session type. Thus an algorithm to compute normal forms
must push all
Dual
operators down the spine of a session type
by applying the conversion rules
C-DualIn
,
C-DualOut
,

C-DualEnd?
, and
C-DualEnd!
from left to
right. If it encounters another
Dual
operator along the
spine, it applies rule
C-DualInv
. We cannot further resolve

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {\alpha}}}
, hence it is a normal form.

The reverse operator only applies to protocol types. So it can only
occur in a message type like
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?(-T)}.{S}}}
and in the parameter of a
protocol type. In a message type, normalization must apply rules

C-NegIn
and
C-NegOut
exhaustively from left to
right, which removes the reverse operator from message types. In the
parameter of a protocol type, normalization applies rule

C-NegInv
exhaustively. Starting with an odd number of
reverse operators, a single operator remains; starting with an even
number, all reverse operators are removed.

Type normalization

Materialization and the directional operators

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-T}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{U}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-T}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-T}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{U}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-T}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

In the second line
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-U}}
in all cases.

Figure 3
:
Type normalization and auxiliary metafunctions on session types and protocol types

Following this intuition normalization is defined in
fig.

3
by two mutually
recursive functions
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\_}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\_}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
along with three
auxiliary functions. Positive normalization
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\_}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
traverses and reconstructs all
non-session type constructs. It also gets invoked on the type of the
transmitted value for session types, i.e., on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
in type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{S}}}
.
Normalizing the
Dual
operator switches forth and back between
positive and negative normalization,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\_}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\_}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
.

Negative normalization with superscript

-
indicates a pending
Dual
type constructor, so that
the function
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\_}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
is only used for session
types. Normalization pushes the pending dual constructor along the spine of a session type to
implement rules
C-DualIn
,
C-DualOut
,

C-DualEnd?
, and
C-DualEnd!
. The pending dualization gets reified on
a type variable.

Normalizing a reversed protocol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-{T}}}
invokes the negative directional
function
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
’s normal form to enforce the
conversion rule
C-DualInv
.

Here is an example of a step-by-step computation of the positive normal form of

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?(-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Int}}})}.{\alpha}}})}}}
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?(-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Int}}})}.{\alpha}}})}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?(-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Int}}})}.{\alpha}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

negative
normalization
remembers pending
Dual

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Int}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\_}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

on the
payload,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\_}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

on the spine

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Int}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {\alpha}}}}}

negative
normalization
reifies

Dual

on type variable

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Int}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {\alpha}}}}}

materialization
applied to normal
form …

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Int}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {\alpha}}}}}}

… fixes the direction

To test whether a type equivalence goal
\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}
holds, we
compute

algorithmic type equivalence
, notation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\equiv_{A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, which is
defined by comparision of normal forms:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
up to
\alpha
-equivalence.
The following propositions establish soundness and
completeness of the normalization algorithm, as well as its worst case running
time.

Theorem 3.1
(Soundness)

Let
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
and
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
.

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}=_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
,
then
\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}=_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
, then
\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}
.

Theorem 3.2
(Completeness)

Let
\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}
.

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}=_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}=_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
.

Theorem 3.3
(Time complexity of type equivalence)

The test for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\equiv_{A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
runs in
O(|{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}|+|{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}|)
.

4
Expressions and Processes

This section introduces the notions of expressions and processes, algorithmic
typing for expressions, and operational semantics for expressions and processes
based on labelled transition relations.
The syntax of constants
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{c}}
, values
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}
, expressions

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}
and processes
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p}}
is defined by the
grammar below.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{c}}\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{fork}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{new}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{receive}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{send}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{wait}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{terminate}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{c}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\lambda{x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}.{e}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{rec}}}\ {x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}.{v}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\Lambda}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{.v}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle v,v\rangle}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{rec}}}\ {x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}.{v}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{receive}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{receive}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{send}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{send}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{send}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}\,v}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e\,e}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{let}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}=e\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{in}}}\,e}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle e,e\rangle}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{let}}}\,\langle x,x\rangle=e\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{in}}}\,{e}}}\;\mid\;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{match}}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{with}}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\{{C}_{i}\;{x}_{i}\rightarrow{e}_{i}\}_{{i}\in{I}}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p}}\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e}\rangle}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p\mid p}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{p}}}

All constructors are standard either in linear functional languages or in
session type languages. From functional languages we find function introduction
and elimination (
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\lambda{x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}.{e}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}\,e_{2}}}
), linear pair introduction
and elimination (
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{let}}}\,\langle x,y\rangle=e_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{in}}}\,{e_{2}}}}
), linear unit
introduction and elimination (
*
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{let}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}=e_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{in}}}\,e_{2}}}
). From session
languages we find most of the constructors in the form of constants, including

fork
to spawn a new thread,
new
to create a new channel,
receive
to
receive a value on a given channel,
send
to send a value on a given channel,

wait
to wait for a channel to be closed,
terminate
to force channel
closing, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{select}}}\,C}}
to exercise a choice on a given
channel indicated by selector name
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}
. The
operator to offer a choice on some channel cannot be captured by a type in our
type language, hence we have made it an expression (
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{match}}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{with}}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\{{C}_{i}\;{x}_{i}\rightarrow{e}_{i}\}_{{i}\in{I}}}}}}
).
At the level of processes, we have expressions as threads
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e}\rangle}}
,
parallel composition
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}\mid p_{2}}}
, and
introduction of a channel by declaring its
two ends
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{y}}
:
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{p}}}
.

Types for constants

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{typeof}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{(}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{c}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{typeof}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{(}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{typeof}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{(}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{fork}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}}})\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{typeof}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{(}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{new}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}{.{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {\alpha}}}}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{typeof}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{(}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{terminate}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{typeof}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{(}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{receive}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}}}}{.{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\beta}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}{.?\alpha.\beta\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\otimes\beta}}}}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{typeof}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{(}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{wait}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{typeof}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{(}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{send}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}}}}{.{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\beta}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}{.\alpha\to{!\alpha}.\beta\to\beta}}}}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{typeof}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{(}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{select}}}\,C_{k}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\overline{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}}}{.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\beta}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}{.{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}\penalty\ {\overline{\alpha}}}})}.{\beta}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T_{k}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}}}}}}}}}}\text{ if }{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{protocol}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}\ \overline{\alpha}}}\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{=}}\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}_{i}}}\ \overline{T_{i}}}\}_{i\in I}}}\text{ and }k\in I

U}}}}\mid\Gamma_{2}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma_{2}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{3}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}\,e_{2}}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}\mid\Gamma_{3}}\quad\textsc{{{E}-{App}} }\quad\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma_{2}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\mid\Gamma_{3}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\otimes U}}}}\mid\Gamma_{3}}\quad\textsc{{{E}-{Pair}} }\par\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\to U}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon^{\!\!\!\star}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V}}}\mid\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon^{\!\!\!\star}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{rec}}}\ {x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\to U}}}}}.{v}}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}\mid\Gamma}\quad\textsc{{{E}-{Rec}} }\par\frac{\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\mid\Gamma_{2}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\Lambda}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{.v}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}{.T}}}}}\mid\Gamma_{2}}\quad\textsc{{{E}-{TAbs}} }\par\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}{.U}}}}}\mid\Gamma_{2}\quad\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}\mid\Gamma_{2}}\quad\textsc{{{E}-{TApp}} }\par\par\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\otimes U}}}}\mid\Gamma_{2}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma_{2},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}\mid\Gamma_{3}\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{y}}\not\in\Gamma_{3}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{let}}}\,\langle x,y\rangle=e_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{in}}}\,{e_{2}}}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}}\mid\Gamma_{3}}\quad\textsc{{{E}-{Let}} }\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{protocol}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}\ \overline{\alpha}}}\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{=}}\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}_{i}}}\ \overline{T_{i}}}\}_{i\in I}}}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}\penalty\ {\overline{U}}}})}.{S}}}}}\mid\Gamma_{2}\quad\forall i\in I:\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma_{2},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{U}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{i}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V_{i}}}\mid\Gamma_{i}\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x_{i}}}\not\in\Gamma_{i}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}=_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V_{i}}}\quad{\Gamma_{3}}=_{\alpha}{\Gamma_{i}}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{match}}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{with}}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\{{C}_{i}\;{x}_{i}\rightarrow{e}_{i}\}_{{i}\in{I}}}}}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}\mid\Gamma_{3}}\quad\textsc{{{E}-{Match}} }

Type against

\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma

\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\mid\Gamma_{2}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}=_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}}\quad\textsc{{{E}-{Check}} }

Figure 5
:
Algorithmic typing rules for expressions

Expression typing is again mostly standard with respect to (linear) functional
or session type languages. The definitions for typing assume a set of
protocol declarations as an implicit argument. The typings of

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{select}}}\,C}}
(
fig.

4
) and
match

(
fig.

5
,
E-Match
) access these protocol declarations.
The
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{typeof}}}
operator in
fig.

4
returns the
types for constants in normal form. The typing rules for expressions are defined
in
fig.

5
.
The type system is algorithmic, implemented with two mutually recursive
judgments: one to synthetize a type of an expression, the other to check an
expression against a type. The typing rules rely on type variable contexts

\Delta
introduced in
section

3
, on term variable contexts
\Gamma

assigning types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
to term variables
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}
. There are two sorts of
entries in term variable contexts: linear entries, noted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}
, and
unrestricted entries, noted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon^{\!\!\!\star}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}
. The latter are used for recursive
functions only (cf. rules
E-Var
\star
and
E-Rec
) and are inspired
by work on quantitative type theory

Atkey 2018
,
McBride 2016
,
Bernardy et al. 2018
.

The type
system maintains the invariant that entries in term variable contexts are always normalized:
the rules that write into contexts, namely

E-Abs
,
E-Rec
and
E-Let
, normalize the
new entry.
Judgments are of the form
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
or

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
, where
\Gamma_{2}
represents the
part of context
\Gamma_{1}

Walker 2005

that is not consumed by
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is in normal form.
The axioms
(
E-Const
and
E-Var
) mostly copy the input context to the
output. Rule
E-App
is a good example of context threading:
to type expression
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}\,e_{2}}}
, expression
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}
is given the
input context
\Gamma_{1}
, produces
\Gamma_{2}
which is given to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}
,
which turn produces
\Gamma_{3}
that constitutes the outgoing context of
application
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}\,e_{2}}}
.
The rules that add linear entries to the context (
E-Abs
,
E-Let
and
E-Match
) all check that the new variables are not present
in the outgoing context, thus ensuring that the corresponding values are fully
consumed.
Rule
E-TApp
explicitly normalizes the output type for normalization is
not preserved by substitution; all rules that introduce new entries in the
context (
E-Abs
,
E-Rec
,
E-Let
, and

E-Match
) make sure that types are normalized, thus ensuring that contexts contain only
normalized types.

Rules
E-Rec
and
E-Var
\star
govern the typing of
recursive functions. Rule
E-Rec
binds the recursive function
x

using an unrestricted binding
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon^{\!\!\!\star}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\_}}}}
so that any number of
recursive calls is possible in the body via
E-Var
\star
. Unrestricted
bindings provide for termination of recursive functions. Such functions cannot use linear variables from the
environment, which we enforce by requiring the incoming context

\Gamma
to be equal to the outgoing context.

The truly new rule is
E-Match
, which indicates that all
rules are parametric with respect to an implicit set of protocol
declarations: there is a declaration for each protocol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}
which
has a kinding in
\Delta
. The rule starts by synthesizing the
input type of the expression to be matched;
the type is

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}\penalty\ {\overline{U}}}})}.{S}}}
for some
protocol constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}
. At this point, the declaration of protocol

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}
is looked up in the implicit set of declarations. The

match
branch for each choice of selector
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C_{i}}}
is then typed in turn. The
parameters
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}
of the protocol are instantiated with
the arguments
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{U}}}
to produce a sequence of types

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{U}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}
. This sequence of
types is converted to a normalized session type as described in
section

3
,
which is assigned to variable
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x_{i}}}
. Now, each branch synthesizes a type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V_{i}}}
and context
\Gamma_{i}
, for
i\in I
. In all branches,
the output types and the contexts must coincide (up to
\alpha

equivalence) with the output type
and context of the
match
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
and
\Gamma_{3}
. In practice,
the implementation selects one branch, say
k\in I
, and compares all
other output types and contexts with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V_{k}}}
and
\Gamma_{k}
.
In this rule, and also in the type for
select
in
fig.

4
, we extend the
directional operators and materialization (from
fig.

3
)
to sequences
of parameters by mapping:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\pm}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}{}=\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\pm}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}{}}
as well as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\overline{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}}
.

Rule
E-Check
relies on the previous judgement to check expression
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}
against
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, up to
\alpha
-equivalence. This simple comparison is
sufficient because synthesis outputs
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
in normal form and
checking must be invoked with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
in normal form.

The operational semantics for expressions and processes is defined via a
transition relation labelled by the below actions.

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\;::=\;\;{x}?{v}\;\mid\;{x}!{v}\;\mid\;{x}?{C}\;\mid\;{x}!{C}\;\mid\;{x?}\;\mid\;{x}!

labels for session operations

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\;::=\;\;\sigma\;\mid\;\beta\;\mid\;\texttt{fork}\,{v}\;\mid\;(\nu{x}{x})\colon{T}

labels for expressions

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle\;::=\;\;\sigma\;\mid\;\tau\;\mid\;(\nu{x}{x}){x}!{x}\;\mid\;({\pi}\mid{\pi})

labels for processes

The
\sigma
labels capture the six operations on sessions:
receive
,

send
,
match
,
select
,
wait
and
terminate
.
Labels
\lambda
are for expressions:
\beta
is for internal actions and the
remaining two for
fork
and
new
.
Labels
\pi
are for processes:
\tau
is for silent actions (the counterpart of

\beta
for expressions),
(\nu{a}{b}){x}!{a}
or
(\nu{a}{b}){x}!{b}
is for opening the
scope of a
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu ab)}}
binding, and
\pi\mid\pi
for parallel
composition.

Labelled transition system for expressions

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\xrightarrow{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\lambda{x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}.{e}}})\,v}}}}\xrightarrow{\beta}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-App}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\Lambda}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{.v}})[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}}}\xrightarrow{\beta}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-TApp}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{let}}}\,\langle x,y\rangle={{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle u,v\rangle}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{in}}}\,{e}}}}}\xrightarrow{\beta}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{u}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Let}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{let}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{in}}}\,e}}}}\xrightarrow{\beta}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Let*}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{({{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{rec}}}\ {x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}.{v}}}})\,u}}}}\xrightarrow{\beta}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{rec}}}\ {x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}.{v}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}})\,u}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Rec}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{fork}}}\,v}}}}\xrightarrow{\texttt{fork}\,{v}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Fork}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{new}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}}}\xrightarrow{(\nu{x}{y})\colon{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle x,y\rangle}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-New}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{receive}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}\,x}}}}\xrightarrow{{x}?{v}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle v,x\rangle}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Rcv}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{send}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}\,v}}\,x}}}}\xrightarrow{{x}!{v}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Send}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{match}}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{with}}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\{{C}_{i}\;{y}_{i}\rightarrow{e}_{i}\}_{{i}\in{I}}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{{x}?{C_{k}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{k}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{y_{k}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Match}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}\,x}}}}\xrightarrow{{x}!{C}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Sel}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{wait}}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}}}\xrightarrow{{x?}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Wait}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{terminate}}}\;{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}}}\xrightarrow{{x}!}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Term}
