We group under label
\beta
the common reduction rules of the polymorphic
lambda calculus. Rules Act-Fork and Act-New record in the label the value forked
and the names and types of the channel ends created. These two transitions will
then be handled at the process level. Then we have the six transitions for the
common session operations.

Labelled transition system for processes

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p}}\xrightarrow{\pi}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p}}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}}}\xrightarrow{\sigma}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e_{1}}\rangle}}}}\xrightarrow{\sigma}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e_{2}}\rangle}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Session}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}}}\xrightarrow{\beta}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e_{1}}\rangle}}}}\xrightarrow{\tau}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e_{2}}\rangle}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Beta}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\texttt{fork}\,{v}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e_{1}}\rangle}}\xrightarrow{\tau}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e_{2}}\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}}}}\rangle}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Fork}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}\xrightarrow{(\nu{x}{y})\colon{T}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e_{1}}\rangle}}\xrightarrow{\tau}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e_{2}}\rangle}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-New}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{q_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{q_{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}\mid p_{2}}}}}\xrightarrow{{\pi_{1}}\mid{\pi_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{q_{1}\mid q_{2}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-JoinL}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{q_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{q_{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}\mid q_{1}}}}}\xrightarrow{\pi}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}\mid q_{1}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-ParL}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{q_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\pi}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{q_{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}\mid q_{1}}}}}\xrightarrow{\pi}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}\mid q_{2}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-ParR}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\pi}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{y}}\not\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({\pi})}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{p_{1}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\pi}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{p_{2}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-Res}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}\xrightarrow{{x}!{a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}\neq{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{a}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{b}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{a}{b})\,{p_{1}}}}}}\xrightarrow{(\nu{a}{b}){x}!{a}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-OpenL}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}\xrightarrow{{x}!{b}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}\neq{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{a}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{b}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{a}{b})\,{p_{1}}}}}}\xrightarrow{(\nu{a}{b}){x}!{b}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-OpenR}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}\xrightarrow{{{x}?{a}}\mid{(\nu{a}{b}){y}!{a}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{p_{1}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\tau}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{a}{b})\,{p_{2}}}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-CloseL}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}\xrightarrow{{{x}?{b}}\mid{(\nu{a}{b}){y}!{b}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{p_{1}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\tau}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{a}{b})\,{p_{2}}}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{Act-CloseR}

Figure 7
:
Labelled transition system for processes

The labelled transition system for processes is defined by the rules in

fig.

7
. The rules are adapted from

Fowler et al. 2021a
,
Fowler et al. 2021b
which in
turn are inspired by
Montesi and Peressotti 2021
. The first four
rules convert expression transitions into process transitions: the session
transitions
\sigma
are passed directly from expressions to processes (rule
Act-Session). The next three rules all yield silent (
\tau
) transitions:
Act-Fork launchs a new thread and Act-New creates a new channel. Rules Act-JoinL
and Act-JoinR account for the commutative nature of parallel composition. Rules
Act-ParL, Act-ParR and Act-Res allow transitions underneath parallel composition
and scope restriction. The rules for opening and closing a scope (left and right
versions) are adapted to the double binder scheme from the labelled transitions
systems for the
\pi
-calculus
Milner et al. 1992
. These
rules are required for we work with free output, unlike the above cited
works
Fowler et al. 2021a
,
Fowler et al. 2021b
,
Montesi and Peressotti 2021

that work with bound output.

Reduction underneath a prefix (rule Act-Res) can only occur if the channels ends

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{y}}
(two bound variables) do not capture the free
variables in the label. For this purpose, we define the free variables of a
process label
\pi
as follows.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({{x}?{v}})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({{x}!{v}})=\{x\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({v})\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({{x}?{C}})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({{x}!{C}})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({{x?}})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({{x}!})=\{x\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({\tau})=\emptyset\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({(\nu{w}{x}){y}!{z}})=\{y,z\}\setminus\{w,x\}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({{\pi}\mid{\pi^{\prime}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({\pi})\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({\pi^{\prime}})

Typing rules for processes

{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p}}

\frac{\cdot\mid\Gamma\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}}}\mid\cdot}{{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{e}\rangle}}}}}\quad\textsc{P-Exp}\par\frac{{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}{\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}}{{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}\mid p_{2}}}}}}\quad\textsc{P-Par}\par\frac{\cdot\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}\quad{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p}}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{p}}}}}}\quad\textsc{P-New}

Figure 8
:
Typing rules for processes

We complete section with the main results of our language.
Towards this end we need to talk about context and process typing.
Type formation (
fig.

1
) is lifted pointwise to contexts in
judgement
{\Delta}\vdash{\Gamma}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
.
The rules for typing processes are in

fig.

8
. The rule for threads requires the expression

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}
to consume all resources given in context
\Gamma
. Rule P-Par splits
the context in two parts and gives each to one of the parallel processes. The
rule is not algorithmic but we plan to type check expressions only; processes
are a runtime artefact. Rule P-New guesses a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
for channel end

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}
, then channel end
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{y}}
gets the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {T}}}
, both in normal
form. Again, this rule is not algorithmic and need not be.
The labelled transitions convey behaviour in their labels. Because we use an
algorithmic typing system with leftovers, we require a labelled transition
system for typing contexts to account for the impact of the behaviour of labels
in the contexts. The statement of preservation relies on the labelled transition
system for contexts, defined in
appendix

0.D
(
fig.

12
).

Theorem 4.1
(Preservation)

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}
and

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{3}
and

{\Gamma_{2}}\xrightarrow{\lambda}{\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}}
and

{\Delta}\vdash{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}
, then

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}^{\prime},\Gamma_{3}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}\mid\Gamma_{3}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}\equiv_{A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}
and

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{3}
and

{\Gamma_{2}}\xrightarrow{\lambda}{\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}}
and

{\Delta}\vdash{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}
, then

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}^{\prime},\Gamma_{3}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{3}
.

3.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\pi}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}
and

{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}
and
{\Gamma_{2}}\xrightarrow{\pi}{\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}}

and
{\cdot}\vdash{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}
,
then
{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}
.

To the below set of labels we call the
progress set
for
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{y}}
:

\{\tau,\,({{x}?{v}}\mid{{y}!{v}}),\,({{x}?{C}}\mid{{y}!{C}}),\,({{x?}}\mid{{y}!}),\,({{x}?{a}}\mid{(\nu{a}{b}){y}!{a}}),\,({{x}?{b}}\mid{(\nu{a}{b}){y}!{b}})\}

Completed processes
are of the form

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}}\rangle}}\mid\dots\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}}\rangle}}}}
.
A process is a
deadlock
if, for all its subterms of the form
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{p}}}

and all transitions
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p}}\xrightarrow{\pi}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p^{\prime}}}
, we have
\pi
not in the progress set
for channel
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{y}}
.
To identify a deadlocked process we find all its
\nu
subterms, and for each
subterm we find all its transitions. Then, if all transitions fall outside the
progress set, the process is deadlocked.
When the kind context
\Delta
is apparent from the context, we write

\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
to denote a typing context composed solely of session types,
that is, such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\in\Gamma
implies
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
.

Theorem 4.2
(Progress possibly leading to deadlock)

1.

If

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
, then either
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}

is a value or
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}
.

2.

If
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
, then either
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}

is a value or
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\lambda}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}
.

3.

If
{\Gamma^{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}
is completed,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}\xrightarrow{\pi}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{2}}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p_{1}}}
is
a deadlock.

5
Implementation

The implementation of AlgST consists of a type checker and an interpreter, both
written in Haskell. Aiming at supporting more realistic programs, the
implementation features pragmatic extensions, including extensions to the
formal system and a simple module system for name space management.

Type checking

The syntax of the implementation closely matches
the examples in this paper. Some places need additional kind annotations
because
the type system of the implementation is not restricted to linear types. Its kind structure distinguishes
between linear and unrestricted variants of the kinds
s
and
t
. Subkinding
allows us to subsume unrestricted versions of types to linear ones, that is,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{un}}}}<{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{lin}}}}
. This subkinding generates a
simple subtyping relation that essentially allows us to provide an unrestricted value where a linear
one is expected. The protocol kind
p
does not split in two versions, but still any type can be lifted to a protocol
type.
Almeida et al. 2022
investigate the metatheory of a system with a
similar kind structure (without the kind
p
).

The type checker implementation closely follows the bidirectional
rules given in the paper. It necessarily contains an implementation of the type
normalization algorithm shown in
fig.

3
. This
algorithm is extended to cater for the type isomorphism
\forall\alpha.(T\to U)\cong T\to\forall\alpha.U
(if
\alpha
not free in
T
). In this way, type applications can
be placed more liberally as long as their sequence is preserved.

The normalization algorithm is invoked exactly as indicated by the
algorithmic typing rules in
fig.

5
. By design, the
rules keep the typing assumptions and the outcome of inference in
normal form to minimize the number of times normalization is invoked.

The type checker includes further checking rules to enable writing some lambda abstractions without
type annotations. These rules are adaptions of well-known rules

Dunfield and Krishnaswami 2021
. For example:

\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\mid\Gamma_{2}\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}\not\in\Gamma_{2}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\lambda{x}.{e}}}}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\to U}}}}\mid\Gamma_{2}}\quad\textsc{{{E}-{Abs'}} }\par\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{2}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma_{2}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\to U}}}}\mid\Gamma_{3}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e_{1}\,e_{2}}}}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}\mid\Gamma_{3}}\quad\textsc{{{E}-{App'}} }

The implementation fully supports the overloaded use of data
constructors as protocol operators. We can perform pattern matching against the constructors of
a
List
datatype and use the same constructors in selecting alternatives of the

!
List
protocol. Moreover, the
case
operator is also overloaded to serve as
the
match
operator.

Interpretation

The interpreter employs
standard techniques for the functional part and for
representing values at run time.
It uses one universal type
with constructors for each type in the language.
Processes in AlgST are mapped to
Haskell threads. A fork in the language is implemented
using
forkIO
in Haskell. The interpreter is invoked recursively
for the new thread.

Communication channels are implemented using shared-memory abstractions from
Haskell’s
Control.Concurrent
library. Synchronous
communication (as in this paper) is implemented
by pairs of
MVar
s, a semaphore-like concurrent datastructure

Jones et al. 1996
that behaves like a buffer of size
one. The implementation has an option to switch to
asynchronous channels using bounded
queues (
TBQueue
) from the
stm
library.

As channels are implemented in shared memory, the

send
primitive is naturally polymorphic. Internally, the interpreter mediates
values of the universal type mentioned above between
send
and

receive
operations.

⬇

protocol

and

type

in

AlgST

syntax

---

)

Benchmarking

We substantiate our claim that replacing a worst-case superlinear algorithm for
type equivalence by a linear one yields actual run-time improvements with an experiment.
We compare AlgST’s type equivalence algorithm with
FreeST, a freely available
FreeST
programming language
containing an implementation of type equivalence for polymorphic
context-free session types

Almeida et al. 2020
,
Almeida et al. 2022
.
As the type checker regularly requires type equivalence checks, the performance
of the equivalence algorithm should be understood as a lower
bound of the type checker performance.

To create a collection of equivalent and non-equivalent test cases, we
implemented a generator of instances of AlgST types.
An instance comprises a set of mutually recursive algebraic protocols and a session type
referring to them. We carefully restrict protocols and types
so that a translation from AlgST instances to FreeST types is
possible: the generator avoids polymorphic and nested recursion and restricts the
occurrences of the negation operator to the top level of protocol
constructor arguments.
For each instance
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, we randomly apply the properties of normalization
to generate
an equivalent AlgST type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
(see
fig.

3
). The pairs

({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}})
constitute our test suite of equivalent types.
Non-equivalent tests are obtained from each
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

by either introducing an additional quantifier, or changing a sub-part of the
type to something of the same kind. Possible
replacements are the quantified type variables and the types built into AlgST,
such as
Int
or
String
. The AlgST type is
translated to a session type in FreeST. Protocols are translated inline at
every point of use as recursive branch or choice types, depending on wether it
appears in a sending or receiving context.
For single constructor types, the translation omits the constructor
tag. The arguments of the constructors are translated into nested
sequences of single interactions. See
fig.

9
for an example.

Benchmarks were run using the gauge package v0.2.5
O’Sullivan (last accessed March 2023
with a
timeout of 2 minutes. AlgST was compiled using GHC 9.2.7, FreeST used GHC 8.10.7.
Each test suite comprises 324 tests. FreeST incurred 69 timeouts for the positive tests and 77 timeouts for the negative tests.

Figure

10
compares the execution time of the AlgST and FreeST type equivalence algorithms
for the equivalent and non-equivalent test cases.
The plots substantiate the differences in the execution time of FreeST and AlgST, distinguishing the
linear evolution of AlgST and
the exponential behavior of FreeST as a function of the number of nodes in the abstract
syntax tree of the AlgST type.

(a)

equivalent test cases

(b)

non-equivalent test cases

Figure 10
:
Execution time of the AlgST and FreeST type equivalence algorithms on the equivalent and non-equivalent test cases, represented in log scale as a function of the number of AlgST nodes in the AST.

6
Related Work

This survey of related work concentrates on binary session types and their
treatment of recursion. Many works in the area concentrate on different aspects
and treat recursion informally as an add-on. In particular, the early work of

Takeuchi et al. 1994
,
Honda 1993
did not consider
recursive types.
The school of session types that takes its foundations from the
sequent calculus for dual intuitionistic logic

Caires and Pfenning 2010
,
Caires et al. 2016

initially focuses on finite behaviors. While
recursion would break the logical consistency of their system, they
consider dependently typed variants that include replication

Pfenning et al. 2011
.

Session type systems with recursion.

Generally, recursive types come in two flavors, equirecursive and
isorecursive
Abadi and Fiore 1996
, and the impact of
these different approaches on type checking is well-studied

Pierce 2002
. They have been found to be equally
expressive
Patrignani et al. 2021
. Most
session type systems in the literature support equirecursive session
types restricted to tail recursion and treat them informally.

Recursive types have first been introduced to session types by

Honda et al. 1998
in the context of
\pi
-calculus. Their
system relies on equirecursion and is syntactically restricted to
tail recursion.

Gay and Hole 1999
,
Gay and Hole 2005
study
subtyping in the same setting using a coinductive definition.

Castagna et al. 2009
consider recursive session types
as infinite regular trees. Their types are tagless in the sense that
choices are made according to the set of accepted values and the viability
of the continuation.
The functional session type calculus of

Gay and Vasconcelos 2010
includes subtyping and equirecursive
types, but the latter are restricted to tail-recursive session types.
Fundamentals of session types
Vasconcelos 2012

proposes a range of
\pi
-calculus-based systems with increasing
complexity. One particular focus is the treatment of replication and unrestricted
channel ends, which requires recursive types. In this work, recursive
types are modeled by infinite regular trees, represented using the
familiar
\mu
notation with contractive, tail recursive bodies.

Toninho et al. 2013
extend the linear-logic based line
of work and focus on the combination of functions and processes
including recursive processes and consequently recursive
types for processes and functions. The examples indicate the equirecursive variant.
Apparently, there is no restriction in the use of recursion and
sequencing is implemented using the tensor
\otimes
.

Toninho et al. 2014
cover a different aspect of
equirecursion. They consider corecursive protocol definitions and
analyze their productivity. The main question they pursue is when a
corecursive protocol produces the next value after finitely many
internal steps.
SILL
Pfenning and Griffith 2015
is a process-based language design that
integrates linear and affine types as well as synchronous and
asynchronous communication. It builds directly on the linear logic interpretation of session
types. Its examples make use of polymorphism and equirecursive types in the form of tail
recursive type equations, neither of which are reflected in the type
theoretical presentation.

Bartoletti et al. 2014
offer a semantic analysis of
session types on the basis of labeled transition systems. Their
system features equirecursion in tail position.

Lindley and Morris 2016
define two
functional session type systems with recursion,
\mu
GV and
\mu
CP, and show that
they are related by typing- and semantics-preserving translations. So
we only compare to
\mu
GV in the following.
\mu
GV is based on
a simply-typed linear lambda calculus extended with notions of recursion and
corecursion. These are defined as least and greatest (respectively) fixed points of
strictly positive functors — analogous to a logically
meaningful version of algebraic (co-) datatypes — with generic fold and
unfold operators. Finally, they lift this isorecursive treatment of
recursive data to the level of session types by encoding branching and
recursion, mapping the corresponding data functors over session
types and wrapping them in promises.
We conclude that
\mu
GV features session types with isorecursion and
that it could be used to encode the simply-typed fragment of AlgST,
but without parameterized data types and without the over
direction. Where
\mu
GV uses promises (i.e., a separate channel) to encode the argument to the
fold of the recursive type (i.e., the constructor arguments of an
algebraic datatype), AlgST transmits the body of the fold on the same
channel, thus avoiding the overhead of channel creation.
Moreover,
\mu
GV is mainly a theoretical contribution
whereas a full implementation of AlgST is available.

Context-free and nested session type systems.

Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
proposed
context-free session types to enable the type-safe encoding of
non-regular protocols like the serialization of tree structures or the
stack protocol from the introduction without encurring the overhead of
session initiation. They changed the asymmetric structure of the
traditional session type syntax
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{S}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{S}}}
, where an
item of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is communicated and the session continues with

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}
,
to a symmetric structure where session types can be built from the
atomic transmissions,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!T}}
, choice and branching,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S_{1}\oplus S_{2}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S_{1}\mathbin{\&}S_{2}}}
, and a monoidal sequential
composition operator
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S_{1};S_{2}}}
with unit
Skip
.
This design,
together with equirecursive sessions, leads to a number of technical
problems. First, types come with a non-trivial equational theory:
sequential composition forms a monoid with unit
Skip
and it
distributes over choice and branching. Second, polymorphic recursion
is needed as a recursive call may have to be instantiated with
different continuation sessions. Third, the interaction between
equirecursion and the omission of the restriction to tail recursion
gives rise to a difficult, but decidable, type equivalence relation.
Subsequent work investigated tractable algorithms for type
equivalence

Almeida et al. 2019
,
Almeida et al. 2020
.

Almeida et al. 2022
integrate context-free
session types with explicit polymorphism in the style of
System F. Recent work extends this approach with higher-order types

Costa et al. 2022
.

Padovani 2017
,
Padovani 2019

proposes a different approach to context-free session types embodied
in a functional session calculus called FuSe
{}
. This calculus uses the
type language of context-free session types, but
sidesteps the problems with the sequencing operator and type
equivalence by introducing a resumption operator
\{e\}_{c}
that
deconstructs a sequence of sessions on channel
c:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S_{1};S_{2}}}
. It does so by retyping the channel to
c:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S_{1}}}
for
evaluating
e
, which must return the same channel after finishing

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S_{1}}}
.
More formally, the resumption obeys some kind of frame rule: if
c:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S_{1}}}\vdash e:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\times{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{1}}}}}
, then

c:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S_{1};S_{2}}}\vdash\{e\}_{c}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\times S_{2}}}
. The
resumption temporarily removes the continuation

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S_{2}}}
from the session type and sticks it back on
afterwards. The type system has means to make sure that the channel returned by
e
is
identical to
c
, which is required for soundness. The type
1

does not signify the end of the session, but rather that some known
part of the session has been processed and the session will be
continued in the context (of the resumption).
The management of the choice and branch operations is very close to
AlgST. FuSe
{}
implements choice by transmitting the constructor tag in
a variant type that selects the different continuations. Its branch
operator matches on the received constructor and thus selects the
desired continuation. However, the handling of protocol constructors
in AlgST includes the construction of the continuation type from the
types and directions given in the protocol declaration. FuSe
{}
has no
equivalent to a protocol declaration nor to the type operator

-
to swap the direction of a transmission.

Padovani 2019
implements
FuSe
{}
in OCaml and demonstrates that context-free session types
are amenable to type inference, if the programmer helps with judicious
placement of resumptions. Support for equirecursion is already built into OCaml’s type inference
engine. It is also shown that subtyping is undecidable.

Das et al. 2021
propose the metatheory of
nested session types featuring parametrized definitions of session types
with nested polymorphic recursion.
At first glance, the nested session type

\texttt{List}[\alpha]=\oplus\{\texttt{nil}\colon\texttt{end},\texttt{cons}\colon\alpha\otimes\texttt{List}[\alpha]\}

is very similar to the algebraic protocol

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{protocol}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\texttt{List}}\penalty\ {\alpha}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{Nil}}}\ |\ {\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{Cons}}}\ \alpha\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{List}}}\ \alpha)
, but
there are significant differences. On the
algebraic side, we can materialize the
List
protocol in a type
that sends or receives a list of integers, while the nested type
List
is
already committed to a concrete direction.
Although type nesting enables to capture the modularity characterizing
algebraic sessions, there are several features that distinguish AlgST from
nested session types:
Das et al. propose a structural and equirecursive, rather than a
nominal, view of type definitions, and interpret types coinductively, rather than inductively.
These features have significant impact on the complexity of the
equivalence problem for nested session types and on its implementation:
Das et al. present a sound but incomplete algorithm for type equivalence.
By considering a nominal interpretation of algebraic session types, we reduced the
complexity of the equivalence problem from
doubly-exponential
Das et al. 2021
to
linear and designed a sound and complete algorithm to verify type
equivalence. The examples in section 2 of

Das et al. 2021
translate into AlgST and type check with our implementation.

Gay et al. 2022
consider equirecursive protocols
defined by different kinds of systems of equations (with and without
parameters). Imposing different restrictions on the parameterization
yields different classes of protocols with decision problems for type
equivalence ranging from polytime to undecidable. Their largest
decidable class of pushdown session types is shown to be equivalent to
nested session types, which we can model with AlgST.

Compared to all competiting systems
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2020
,
Almeida et al. 2022
,
Padovani 2019
,
Das et al. 2021
, AlgST’s materialization and
directional operators are unique. These
operators enable the construction of protocols without committing to a
particular direction.

Linear and unrestricted types.

Our
calculus is mostly linear, except that we
allow unrestricted bindings for recursive functions, which would be
rendered useless otherwise.
Our implementation goes well beyond that in
allowing fully fledged unrestricted computations besides ensuring that
session typed channels are treated linearly.
There is some work on recursion and linearity in the context of lambda calculus

Alves et al. 2010

adding iterators on
linear natural numbers to the calculus, thereby avoiding the need for
a rule like
E-Var
\star
.

System F°
Mazurak et al. 2010

extends System F with kinds to integrate polymorphism with linear and
unrestricted types. This work serves as a blueprint for much
subsequent work in this area including LFST and FreeST (see below), as well as our
implemented language.
They categorize all objects, including functions, into one-use or
many-use resources. This style can be traced back to

Walker 2005
.
LFST
Lindley and Morris 2017
formalizes the
session type system of Links, which features a mix of linear and
unrestricted types, embedding a standard functional programming
language. The substructural behavior of types is specified by a
sophisticated kind structure. The kinding used in our implementation
is inspired by LFST, though LFST has additional kinds to describe row
types that we do not support.

Almeida et al. 2022

consider an integration of context-free session types with
System F. Their type system includes a kinding discipline that manages
linear and unrestricted versions of ordinary types and
message types (possible payloads for the send and receive
operations). Our implemented system extends the kind structure in a
similar way.

Linear Haskell (LH)
Bernardy et al. 2018

is a proposal to integrate linear types with stock functional
programming. While the work discussed so far characterizes resources,
LH supports the lollipop type
S\multimap T
which classifies
functions that use their argument exactly once. Originally, LH forced
the programmer to allocate resources using continuations. This restriction
has been fixed in subsequent work
Spiwack et al. 2022
.
There is further work on integrating linear and dependent types (e.g.,

Krishnaswami et al. 2015
) which relies on
bifurcating the language in two calculi connected by an adjunction.

7
Conclusion

Algebraic protocols combine naming and recursion with choice and sequence type constructors known from session types. Like algebraic datatypes,
they offer parameterization and mutual recursion. Unlike previous work on
session types, algebraic
protocols do not commit to a particular direction of communication,
even if direction reversal can be specified at the type level. Algebraic protocols
and session types subsume the expressive power of context-free and nested
session type systems while reducing the complexity of type checking
from doubly-exponential to linear time.

Software Availability

The proposed system and implementation are publicly available as an artifact
Spaderna et al. 2023
. The interested reader can follow the steps in the artifact documentation to set up a pre-built docker image or a self-built docker image. Besides the implementation of the type checker and the interpreter, we provide programs for all the examples in the paper and examples that establish a comparison with related work
Das et al. 2021
,
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
. In the artifact, the reader can also find instructions on how to reproduce our benchmarking results for type equivalence.

Supplement 0.A
Frequently Asked Questions

0.A.1
Session types vs. protocol types

A session type can be assigned to a channel end to describe
the entire communication behavior of this channel up to its closing. Thus, a
session type classifies a run-time value, namely a channel end.
A protocol type does not correspond to any run-time value. It
describes composable behavior for bidirectional communication. A protocol
type becomes part of a session type by binding it to a particular
direction with the
!
and
?
operators.

In a sense, a protocol type describes a composable part of a
communication protocol or the difference between two session
types. This view is emphasized by our pervasive use of channel passing
style.

0.A.2
Negated recursion

It is possible to define an algebraic protocol that negates the polarity of its recursive use.
Such a protocol is fine, though unusual as it changes
direction with every unfolding of the recursion. Here is an example:

⬇

protocol

Flipper

=

Flipper

-
Int

-
Flipper

A server for
Flipper
can be implemented with a pair of
mutually recursive functions or even with just one function:

⬇

flipper

:

!
Flipper
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}

\rightarrow

Unit

flipper

c

=

let

c

=

select

Flipper

[
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
]

c

in

let

(
x
,

c
)

=

receive

[
Int
,

?
Flipper
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
]

c

in

match

c

with

{

Flipper

c

\rightarrow

send

[
Int
,

!
Flipper
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
]

x

c

\rhd

flipper

}

0.A.4
Duality vs. polarities

The
Dual
operator works on
session types
and it inverts
the direction of communication from the outside. Intuitively, the

Dual
operator traverses the spine of a session type and
flips all
!
to
?
and vice versa. It does
not
enter the
type of the message “payload”. For example (with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
the
payload type and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}
the continuation session):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{S}}})}}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {S}}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{S}}})}}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {S}}}}}}

In contrast, the polarity operator
-
works on

protocol types
and inverts the direction of communication from
the inside. This inversion happens at the boundary where a protocol
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}
is turned into a payload type for a session:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-{P}}})}.{S}}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!P}.{S}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-{P}}})}.{S}}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?P}.{S}}}

The soundness of the type system guarantees that the direction of communication is
finally settled by the time we get to a primitive sending or receiving operation.

Like
Dual
, the
-
operator is involutory:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {S}}})}}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-{T}}})}}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

0.A.5
Recursion and duality

Bernardi and Hennessy 2014
,
Bernardi and Hennessy 2016
discovered a problem in the interaction
between duality and recursive session types, which is exposed most
concisely by the recursive type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T=\mu X.!X.X}}
.
The dual of this type is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {(\mu X.!X.X)}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu X.{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?(\mu X.!X.X)}.{X}}}}}
, which can be seen by dualizing the type’s
expansion

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {(\mu X.!X.X)}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {(!(\mu X.!X.X).(\mu X.!X.X))}}}\\
={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?(\mu X.!X.X)}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {(\mu X.!X.X)}}}}}}

Using equations we could define the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{T}}}}}
.
Translated to algebraic session types, the protocol underlying
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is as follows:

⬇

protocol

X

=

Mu

T

X

type

T

=

!
X
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}

selectMu

:

T

\rightarrow

!
T
.
T

selectMu

c

=

select

Mu

[
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}
]

dualT

Dual

\rightarrow

?

.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}

This behavior matches exactly the outcome for

. The function

0.A.6
Efficiency of generic servers

In
section

2.3
, we explore an example with generic servers
to showcase the expressivity of the proposed parametric protocols.
The example has a significant overhead as there are many tagging
operations compared to a manually crafted version.

The present work is a first step and does not offer a solution
supported by formal theorems. But, let us call a protocol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}

tagless
if it is non-recursive and declares a single
constructor tag
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}\ T_{1}\dots T_{n}
. In analogy to Haskell’s

newtype
, an implementation can reduce the
run-time cost of a tagless protocol to zero by omitting the select and
match operations.

0.A.7
Equirecursive vs. isorecursive session types

Most work on session types is using equirecursive types for two reasons. One is historic:

Gay and Hole 1999
,
Gay and Hole 2005
were the first
to study coinductively defined relations on (tail-) recursive session types. They defined the (by now)
standard notion of subtyping and showed that it is
decidable. Subsequent work has built on top of their results. The
other reason is that “The equirecursive interpretation of a session type guarantees that no
message is required for the unfolding of a recursive type.” (
Balzer and Pfenning 2017
)

Algebraic protocols conflate the unfolding of the recursion with
the branching, just like algebraic datatypes do.
For protocols that have finite runs, algebraic protocol do not cause extra messages.
As such protocols use recursion guarded by branching, a
message to decide the branching is needed anyway.

The protocol
Stream

a
in
section

2.3
does cause
extra messages as it (roughly) corresponds to an isorecursive reading of the type
\mu X.!a.X
.

0.A.8
Subtyping, type classes, type inference

Subtyping is undecidable for context-free session types

Padovani 2019
. However, as AlgST’s session
typing relies mostly on nominal types, there is scope for decidable
subtyping. We have a concrete proposal, but it is not implemented
and its metatheory has not been checked, yet.

The types
Send

a
and
Recv

a
for sending and
receiving data of type
a
in

look like types for a dictionary in a language with type classes or
traits

Hall et al. 1996
,
Klabnik et al. 2018
.
In future work we plan to explore the addition of implicit arguments as
in Scala
Odersky et al. 2018
.

Type inference is future work.

0.A.9
Settling of direction

It might be confusing to see a type like
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{S}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!T}.{S}}}
, but
with the possibility that the direction changes later on. However,
this behavior is completely predictable. As long as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a
protocol type variable
\alpha:{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}
, the direction can change
because
\alpha
could be substituted by some protocol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-{T^{\prime}}}}
.
As soon as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is an ordinary type of kind
t
(even if

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a variable), the direction is fixed. For example, in the
type of primitive send

\forall
(
a
:
M
).

\forall
(
s
:
S
).

a

\rightarrow

!
a
.
s

\rightarrow

s
, the payload type

a
has kind
m
, so the direction is fixed.

\frac{\frac{\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{a}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{a}}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{-a}}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}\quad\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{a}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{a}}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}\quad\frac{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Stack}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}\in\Delta\hskip 7.3612pt\frac{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{a}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{a}}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Stack\penalty\ a}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}{\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Stack\penalty\ a}}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}{\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Stack}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}

Lemma 1
(Properties of the materialization and directional operators)

1.

\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}})}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {S}}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}

Lemma 2
(Agreement for type conversion)

If
\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}
, then
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
and

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
.

Proof

By rule induction on the hypothesis.

We start by specifying the syntax of types in normal form. Normal forms for well-formed types
are
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Q}}
as defined by the following grammar:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Q}}

\displaystyle\;::=\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{R}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{-R}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{R}}

\displaystyle\;::=\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{R\to R}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{R\otimes R}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}{.R}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?R}.{R}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{!R}.{R}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{End}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {\alpha}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rho}}}\penalty\ {\overline{Q}}}}

Proposition 1
(Kind preservation)

1.

For all
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
,
\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}
.

2.

For all
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
,
\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Dual}}}\penalty\ {T}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}
.

Proof

See Agda development
nf-sound+
and
nf-sound-
.

Proof
(Proof of
theorem

3.1
(Soundness))

Item

1
. Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}=_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
. From

proposition

1
, we have
\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}
and
\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}
. Conclude by symmetry
and transitivity of conversion as type equality is in
\equiv
.
The proof for
item

2
is analogous.

To obtain a complexity bound for the computation of a normal form,
we exploit its syntactic form.

Lemma 3

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\pm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
is described by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Q}}
.

Proof

See Agda development
nf-normal
.

Proposition 2

For each
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
,
the worst case running time of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\pm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
is in
O(|{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}|)
.

Proof

A run of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\pm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
visits each of the
|{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}|
nodes in the input type
once. At each node, it either reconstructs the type from the normal forms of
the sub-terms in
O(1)
time, or (in the transmission rules) it applies
polarity correction to normal forms. By
Lemma
3
, a normal form starts with at
most one negative polarity, so correction takes at most two steps, at cost

O(1)
.

Proof
(Proof of
theorem

3.3
(Time complexity of type equivalence))

A corollary of
lemma

3
.

Lemma 4

1.

If
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
and
{\Delta}\vdash{\Gamma_{1}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}
,
then
{\Delta}\vdash{\Gamma_{2}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is in normal form.

2.

If
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
and
{\Delta}\vdash{\Gamma_{1}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is in normal form, then
{\Delta}\vdash{\Gamma_{2}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}
.

Proof

1.

By rule induction on the first hypothesis.

2.

Using
item

1
and observing that a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}=_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is also in normal form.

Lemma 5
(Properties of normalization)

1.

If
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
, then

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
.

2.

If
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
, then

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
.

3.

If
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
, then

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
and
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
.

4.

If
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\pm}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
,
then
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
.

Proof

The proof is by mutual induction on the various hypotheses. Most cases follow
by a straightforward induction. We detail the case for

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{S}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathsection(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{).}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
.

Item

3
gives

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
and

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
. Rule
T-Sub
gives

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
and

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
.
Item

4
gives

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
. Again rule
T-Sub
gives

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
.
Item

1
gives

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
. Once again, rule
T-Sub
gives

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
. From
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
, induction
gives
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
and
T-Sub
gives

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
. Finally, rule
T-In
gives

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{S}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
and rule
T-Sub
yields

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{?T}.{S}}}}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
.

Lemma 6
(Substitution)

1.

If
\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}
and
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
, then

\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}
.

2.

If
\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
and
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
,
then
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
.

3.

If
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
and

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{3}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\mid\cdot
, then

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{3}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
.

4.

If
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon^{\!\!\!\star}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon^{\!\!\!\star}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}
and

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{3}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\mid\cdot
, then

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{3}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
.

Proof

The proof follows by mutual induction on the first hypothesis.

Lemma 7
(Agreement)

Let
{\Delta}\vdash{\Gamma_{1}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\textbf{{t}}}}}
.

1.

If
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
, then
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
.

2.

If
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
, then
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
.

3.

If
{\Gamma_{1},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p}}
, then
\cdot\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}
.

Proof

1.

By rule induction on the first hypothesis.

2.

By rule induction on the first hypothesis, using
lemma

6
and
lemma

2
.

3.

By rule induction on the first hypothesis, using
E-Check
and
item

1
.

Monotonicity tells us that resources are not created during the process of type
checking.

Lemma 8
(Monotonicity)

If
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
, then
\Gamma_{2}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}
.

Proof

A straightforward rule induction on the hypothesis. The base cases are E-Const
and E-Var. The cases for E-Abs, E-TAbs, E-Pair and E-TApp follow by induction
and transitivity of the subset relation.

Lemma 9
(Strengthening)

1.

If
\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}\not\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{fv}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}})
,
then
\Delta\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}
.

2.

If
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}
, then

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
.

Proof

1.

The proof is by induction hypothesis on the first hypothesis, using
T-Sub
.

2.

The proof is by induction hypothesis on the first hypothesis.

Lemma 10
(Weakening)

If
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
, then

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}
.

Proof

The proof follows by induction on the hypothesis.

Preservation and progress

We now prove the main results.

Proof
(Proof of
theorem

4.1
(Preservation))

The proof follows by mutual rule induction on the first hypothesis.

1.

Rule Act-App. In this case,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\lambda{x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}.{e}}})\,v}}}}\xrightarrow{\beta}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}

and
\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}=\cdot
. Inversion of
E-App
on

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{3}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\lambda{x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}.{e}}})\,v}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\mid\Gamma_{3}

yields (a)
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{3}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\lambda{x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}.{e}}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V\to T}}}}\mid\Gamma^{\prime}

and
(b)
\Delta\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}\mid\Gamma_{3}
. By monotonicity (
lemma

8
), we
know that
\Gamma_{3}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
; say that
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},\Gamma_{3}
.
Applying inversion of
E-Abs
on (a) and
strengthening (
lemma

9
) on (b), we get

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{3},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},\Gamma_{3}
and

\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}\mid\cdot
(and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
). By
C-Refl
and
agreement (
lemma

7
) on the second hypothesis, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\equiv_{A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
.
Conclude applying the substituion lemma
(
lemma

6
,
item

3
) and weakening (

).

Rule Act-TApp. Again,
\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}=\cdot

on the

and conclude
with

,

,

3

Rule Act-Let*. In this case,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{let}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{in}}}\,e}}}}\xrightarrow{\beta}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{e}}}}

\Gamma_{1}=\cdot

, as we wanted.

Rule Act-New. In this case,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{new}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}}}\xrightarrow{(\nu{x}{y})\colon{U}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle x,y\rangle}}}}
.
By L-New, we know that
\Gamma_{2}=\cdot
and
\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}}
.
Inversion of
E-TApp
and
E-Const
on the second hypothesis yields

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{nrm}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{-}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
and
\Gamma_{1}=\cdot
. Using

E-Var
and

we conclude that

Rule Act-Rcv. In this case,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{receive}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}\,x}}}}\xrightarrow{{x}?{v}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle v,x\rangle}}}}

Canonical forms tell us the form of values from the form of types.

Lemma 11
(Canonical forms)

Let
\Delta\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid\Gamma_{2}
.

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\texttt{Unit}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{*}}}
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U\to V}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\lambda{x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}.{e}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{rec}}}\ {x}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}.{e}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{fork}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{wait}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{terminate}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{receive}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{send}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{send}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}\,u}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{W}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}
.

3.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U\otimes V}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\langle u,w\rangle}}
.

4.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}}{.U}}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\Lambda}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\kappa}}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{.u}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{receive}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{receive}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{send}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{send}}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{v}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\texttt{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{C}}[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{V}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{]}}
or

Lemma 12
(Completed processes are closed)

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p}}
is completed, then
{\cdot}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{p}}
.
